Cellwise and Casewise Robust Multivariate Regression with Inference
Fabio Centofanti, Mia Hubert, Peter J. Rousseeuw

Abstract
Multivariate linear regression is a fundamental statistical task, but classical estimators such as ordinary least squares are highly sensitive to outliers. These may occur as casewise outliers that affect entire observations, or as outlying cells, that are individual contaminated entries in the predictor and/or response matrix. Moreover, modern datasets frequently contain missing values and are high-dimensional. To address these challenges we propose the cellwise multivariate regression (cellMR) estimator, a robust regression method that simultaneously accommodates casewise and cellwise outliers, missing data, and high dimensionality. The approach builds on a cellwise robust covariance estimator and uses ridge regularization for numerical stability. We further introduce cellBoot, a novel bootstrap-based inference procedure tailored to the cellMR framework. Relying on indirect inference, cellBoot provides asymptotically valid confidence intervals that are robust to casewise and cellwise contamination. We derive influence functions of the regression estimator and prove the asymptotic validity of the cellBoot confidence intervals. Simulations and a real genomics application illustrate the strong finite-sample performance of the proposed methods.



Keywords:

Anomaly detection;
Casewise outliers;
Cellwise outliers;
Confidence intervals;

Indirect
Inference.

1
Introduction

Multivariate linear regression is a fundamental
tool in statistics that models the relationship
between
p
-dimensional predictors
\bm{x}_{i}
and

q
-dimensional responses
\bm{y}_{i}
from a sample

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. It plays a central
role in a wide range of scientific disciplines, as
it provides an interpretable framework for
understanding complex multivariate relationships and
for making predictions. The regression model is
typically fit by the ordinary least squares (OLS)
method, that minimizes a squared loss criterion.

However, nowadays people routinely collect large
and complex datasets, that are often
contaminated by outliers, also called anomalies.
Those are parts of the data that deviate markedly
from the majority.
They may arise from a variety of sources, such as
measurement errors, data entry mistakes, sensor
malfunctions, or rare and unexpected events.
Because OLS is highly sensitive to such
contamination, its performance can deteriorate
substantially.
It can be attracted by outliers so strongly that
its residuals hide the outliers. This
is called the masking effect. Additionally, some
regular values might even appear to be outlying,
which is known as swamping.
The paradigm of robust statistics
(
Huber 1981
;
Hampel et al. 1986
;
Rousseeuw and Leroy 1987
;
Maronna et al. 2019
)
provides
a strategy to mitigate these effects. Robust
methods produce estimates that are only mildly
affected by the presence of outliers.
The outliers can then be detected by their
large deviations from the estimated model.

Research on outliers has traditionally focused on
outlying cases, also known as casewise outliers.
These are observations
(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
that were
not generated by the same underlying mechanism as the
majority of the data. Many robust regression methods
have been developed to address casewise outliers
in order to detect and downweight them, see e.g.

Rousseeuw and Leroy 1987
and Chapters 4–5 of

Maronna et al. 2019
. All casewise robust methods
require that at least 50% of the couples

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
are clean.

In recent years, increasing attention has been
devoted to cellwise outliers
(
Alqallaf et al. 2009
)
.
In the context of the linear regression model,
these correspond to anomalous cells (entries) in
the combined matrix
[\bm{X}\,;\,\bm{Y}]
of predictors
and responses. Cellwise outliers might only
contaminate a few coordinates of a case

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
. They are particularly common in
high-dimensional datasets, that is, with a large
number of predictor variables and/or response
variables. Even a relatively small proportion of
outlying cells can contaminate many cases.
When random cells in the predictors and responses
are contaminated with probability
\varepsilon
, the
expected fraction of contaminated cases is

1-(1-\varepsilon)^{(p+q)}
. This grows fast with
p

and
q
: even if only 1% of the cells is
contaminated with
p=100
and
q=2
, we can expect
64% of the cases to be contaminated. In such
situations casewise robust methods become ineffective.

Raymaekers and Rousseeuw 2026
reviewed
the challenges of dealing with cellwise outliers.
Several proposals for cellwise robust regression
with
q=1
have been made. One of the earliest
was the shooting S-estimator of

Öllerer and Croux 2015
, that iteratively updates
each coefficient by a regression with
p=1
.
Afterward
Bottmer et al. 2022
constructed
a sparse version of the shooting S-estimator.

Leung et al. 2016
proposed an alternative
approach that is based on robust estimation of the
joint location and scatter of the pairs

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
using the 2SGS estimator of

Agostinelli et al. 2015
.
The cellwise robust M-regression estimator
of
Filzmoser et al. 2020
relies on an
iteratively reweighted least squares procedure.
More recently,
Su et al. 2024
introduced a
regularization approach that minimizes both a
regression loss and a cell deviation measure.

Contemporary applications increasingly involve
situations in which the number of predictors
p

is comparable to, or even
exceeds, the sample size
n
. In such regimes,
classical estimation procedures may become unstable
or ill-posed, and some regularization is required.
Several casewise robust approaches for regularized
regression have been proposed, including an
MM-estimator with a ridge penalty

(
Maronna 2011
)
and robust elastic net
methods
(
Cohen Freue et al. 2019
)
.
See
Filzmoser and Nordhausen 2021
for a
comprehensive review. A further challenge arises
from missing data
(
Little 1992
)
,
that is common in real-world applications and
may occur together with
contamination. Most robust procedures were
developed for fully observed data. So far there
was no multivariate regression method capable of
jointly addressing cellwise contamination, high
dimensionality, and missing data.

An advantage of classical multivariate linear
regression is the availability of inferential
procedures. Inference enables uncertainty
quantification for regression coefficients, the
construction of confidence regions, and
hypothesis testing for linear contrasts or groups
of parameters. In many applications the objective
is not only prediction, but also reliable
scientific interpretation, which requires an
accurate assessment of sampling variability.

Inference based on robust estimators is
substantially more challenging. One approach is
based on the asymptotic distribution of robust
estimators. Those are typically derived under
elliptical assumptions, and
rely on normal approximations with estimated
asymptotic covariance matrices. However, such
assumptions rarely hold. The bootstrap offers an
attractive alternative without relying heavily
on distributional assumptions, see

Efron and Tibshirani 1994
.
It repeatedly draws samples with replacement from
the observed cases, and recalculates the estimator
on each resample. The empirical distribution of
these estimates then approximates the estimator’s
distribution. This approach can be extended to
robust regression settings.
For casewise contamination, robust bootstrap
procedures have been developed along two main
directions. The first is the fast and robust
bootstrap of
Salibian-Barrera and Zamar 2002
,
originally proposed for simple regression and
later extended to multivariate regression

(
Van Aelst and Willems 2005
)
. It provides an
asymptotically consistent and
computationally efficient framework for robust
inference, reviewed in
Salibián-Barrera et al. 2008
.
A second line of work, initiated by

Amado and Pires 2004
, modifies the resampling
scheme by assigning lower sampling probabilities
to potentially harmful cases.

In this paper we propose a cellwise multivariate
regression method called cellMR that
simultaneously accommodates cellwise and
casewise contamination, missing values, and
high-dimensional settings. To the best of our
knowledge, it is the first method to do so.
It builds on a recent cellwise robust
covariance estimator of

Centofanti et al. 2025
, and uses ridge
regularization to enhance stability in high
dimensions. We moreover derive the cellwise and
casewise influence functions of cellMR.

We complement cellMR with a novel inference
procedure, termed the
cellwise bootstrap

(cellBoot). This nonparametric method
adopts indirect inference (II), a
simulation-based bias correction approach that
constructs a consistent estimator from an
inconsistent but computationally efficient
auxiliary estimator

(
Gourieroux et al. 1993
;
Guerrier et al. 2019
)
.
The auxiliary estimator only requires a single
run of cellMR. As far as we know, cellBoot is
the first inference procedure designed to
handle cellwise and casewise contamination
combined with missing values.
We were able to prove the asymptotic
consistency of the cellBoot procedure.
We also obtain influence functions of the
center and the length of its confidence
intervals.

Section
2
introduces the cellMR
method. Section
3

constructs graphical displays to facilitate
outlier detection.
Section
4
describes the
cellBoot inference procedure. The finite-sample
performance of cellMR and cellBoot is evaluated
through simulation in Section
5
, and
Section
6
illustrates them on a
real dataset from genomics.
Section
7
concludes.

2
Robust multivariate regression by cellMR

Let us consider a random sample

\{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
where
\bm{x}_{i}
denotes the predictor vector and

\bm{y}_{i}
the response vector. The multivariate
linear regression model is given by

\bm{y}_{i}=\bm{b}+\bm{B}^{T}\bm{x}_{i}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(1)

where the
q
-dimensional vector
\bm{b}
is the
intercept,
\bm{B}
is the
p\times q
slope matrix,
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{1},\dots\bm{\varepsilon}_{n}
are
q
-dimensional
random errors with zero mean and covariance

\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}
independent of
\bm{x}_{i}
.
Denote by
\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{i}
the
d
-dimensional vector

\left(\bm{x}_{i}^{T},\bm{y}_{i}^{T}\right)^{T}
. The sample
mean and empirical covariance matrix of the

\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{i}
can be partitioned as

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{x}\\[2.0pt]
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{y}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x}&\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{xy}\\[2.0pt]
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{yx}&\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{y}\end{pmatrix}.

Under model (
1
), the classical
ridge regression is given by

\bm{\widehat{B}}=(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x}+\lambda\bm{I}_{p})^{-1}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{xy}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bm{\widehat{b}}=\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{y}-\bm{\widehat{B}}^{T}\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{x}\,,

(2)

with estimated error covariance matrix

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}=\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{y}-\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{yx}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x}+\lambda\bm{I}_{p})^{-1}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{xy}\,.

(3)

The advantage of the regularization by

\lambda\geqslant 0
is that it avoids the
inversion of a potentially ill-conditioned
matrix
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x}
that can occur due to
multicollinearity or a high dimension
p
,
possibly even with
p>n
. The tuning parameter

\lambda
is typically chosen by
cross-validation.

However, the sample mean and covariance matrix
can be much affected by outliers, leading
to unreliable coefficients.

2.1
The cellMR estimator

Formulas (
2
) and (
3
)
allow to obtain other regression estimators by
plugging in suitable estimators of the location
\bm{\mu}

and scatter
\bm{\Sigma}
of the
\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{i}
. For this we
turn to the cellwise robust covariance estimator
of
Centofanti et al. 2025
that performs well
under both casewise and cellwise outliers, even in
high dimensions. We will focus on its unregularized
version that we denote by cellCov, since we will
regularize the regression
by (
2
)–(
3
)
afterward anyway.

To obtain the cellCov estimator, the combined

n\times d
data matrix

\bm{\widetilde{Z}}=\{\widetilde{z}_{ij}\}=\left[\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{n}\right]^{T}
is first
standardized to
\bm{Z}=\{z_{ij}\}=(\bm{z}_{1},\dots,\bm{z}_{n})^{T}=\bm{\widetilde{Z}}\bm{\widehat{D}}^{-1}
, where

\bm{\widehat{D}}=\diag(\widehat{\sigma}^{\widetilde{Z}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\sigma}^{\widetilde{Z}}_{d})
. Here

\widehat{\sigma}^{\widetilde{Z}}_{j}:=\sigma_{M}(\{\widetilde{z}_{ij}-m_{j}\}_{i=1}^{n})
, where

m_{j}
is the median of the
j
-th variable and

\sigma_{M}
is the robust scale M-estimator
defined in Section
A
of the
Supplementary Material.

Next, cellCov applies the cellPCA method

(
Centofanti et al. 2026
)
, a robust principal component
analysis (PCA) method designed to handle both
cellwise and casewise outliers as well as
missing values. We model the matrix

\bm{Z}
as

\bm{Z}=\bm{1}_{n}\bm{\mu}_{z}^{T}+\bm{U}\bm{V}^{T}+\bm{E},

(4)

where
\bm{1}_{n}
is a column vector with all

n
components equal to
1
, the scores
matrix
\bm{U}=\{u_{i\ell}\}=\left[\bm{u}_{1},\dots,\bm{u}_{n}\right]^{T}
is

n\times k
, the loadings matrix

\bm{V}=\{v_{j\ell}\}=\left[\bm{v}_{1},\dots,\bm{v}_{d}\right]^{T}
is
d\times k
, and the matrix

\bm{E}=\left[\bm{z}^{\perp}_{1},\dots,\bm{z}^{\perp}_{n}\right]^{T}

is the noise term. The cellPCA method obtains
estimates
\bm{\widehat{V}}
,
\bm{\widehat{U}}
, and
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}
by
minimizing the loss function

L_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(\bm{Z},\bm{V},\bm{U},\bm{\mu}_{z}):=\frac{\widehat{\sigma}_{2}^{2}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\rho_{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{\sigma}_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}m_{ij}\,\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}^{2}\,\rho_{1}\!\left(\frac{r_{ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}}\right)}\,\right),

(5)

where the
r_{ij}:=z_{ij}-\mu_{z,j}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}u_{i\ell}v_{j\ell}
, the
missing value indicator
m_{ij}
is 0 if

x_{ij}
is missing and 1 otherwise,

m_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}m_{ij}
,
and
m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}
.
The scale
\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}:=\sigma_{M}(\{r_{ij}\}_{i=1}^{n})

standardizes the
cellwise PCA residual

r_{ij}
, and

\widehat{\sigma}_{2}:=\sigma_{M}(\{t_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})

divides the
casewise total deviation

t_{i}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}m_{ij}\,\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}^{2}\,\rho_{1}\!\left(\frac{r_{ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}}\right)}.

(6)

For
\rho_{1}(z)=\rho_{2}(z)=z^{2}
the
objective (
5
) becomes
the objective of classical PCA.
But instead cellPCA uses functions

\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
that are valid
in our framework.

Definition 1
.

A function

\rho:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is called a valid
\rho
-function if it is
continuous and differentiable, even, bounded,
nondecreasing in
|z|
, has
\rho(0)=0
,
and is such that the mapping

z\mapsto\rho(\sqrt{z})
is concave for

z\geqslant 0
.

The cellPCA method uses the valid hyperbolic
tangent
\rho
-function
(
Hampel et al. 1986
)
, that
is described in the Supplementary
Material
A
. This makes

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}
,
\bm{\widehat{U}}
, and
\bm{\widehat{V}}
robust against
both cellwise and casewise outliers.
Indeed, a cellwise outlier in the cell
(i,j)

yields a cellwise PCA residual
r_{ij}
with a
large absolute value, but the boundedness of

\rho_{1}
reduces its effect on the estimates.
Similarly, a casewise outlier results in a large
casewise total deviation
t_{i}
but its effect
is reduced by
\rho_{2}
.
Note that in the computation of
t_{i}
the effect
of cellwise outliers is tempered by the presence
of
\rho_{1}
. This avoids that a single very
outlying cell could give its case a large
t_{i}
.

Define the matrix
\bm{\widehat{Z}}=\{\widehat{z}_{ij}\}=\left[\bm{\widehat{z}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\widehat{z}}_{n}\right]^{T}
, where

\bm{\widehat{z}}_{i}=\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}+\bm{\widehat{V}}\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i}
are the fitted points
in the
k
-dimensional principal subspace. Then
estimate their location and scatter by

\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\bm{z}^{k}}=\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}+\bm{\widehat{V}}\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{k}}=\bm{\widehat{V}}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})\bm{\widehat{V}}^{T},

(7)

where

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})
and

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})
are the MCD estimates of

\bm{\widehat{u}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\widehat{u}}_{n}

(
Rousseeuw 1984
;
Hubert et al. 2012
)
. This avoids undue
influence of outlying
\bm{\widehat{z}}_{i}
.

Then we define
\bm{\widetilde{W}}=\bm{W}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\odot\bm{M}
, where
the Hadamard product
\odot
multiplies matrices
entry by entry, and where the
n\times d
matrix

\bm{W}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}=\{w_{ij}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\}

contains the
cellwise PCA weights

w_{ij}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\frac{\widehat{r}_{ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}}\right)=\psi_{1}\!\left(\frac{\widehat{r}_{ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}}\right)\Big/\frac{\widehat{r}_{ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}},\quad i=1,\dots,n,\quad j=1\dots,d,

(8)

where
\widehat{r}_{ij}=z_{ij}-\widehat{z}_{ij}
and

\psi_{1}=\rho_{1}^{\prime}
with the convention

w_{ij}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(0)=1
.
The
n\times d
matrix
\bm{M}
contains the
missingness indicators
m_{ij}
.
We then estimate the scatter in the
orthogonal complement of the principal
subspace as

\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}=\{\widetilde{{\sigma}}_{(\bm{z}^{\perp})j\ell}\}=\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{i}(\bm{z}_{i}-\bm{\widehat{z}}_{i})(\bm{z}_{i}-\bm{\widehat{z}}_{i})^{T}\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{i},

(9)

where
\bm{\widetilde{W}}_{i}
is a diagonal matrix
whose diagonal is the
i
-th row of
\bm{\widetilde{W}}
,
and
b
is given by

b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}m_{ij}m_{i\ell}w_{i}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}w_{ij}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}w_{i\ell}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}/d^{2}
.
The
casewise PCA weights

w_{i}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}

are defined as

w_{i}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\frac{\widehat{t}_{i}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{2}}\right)=\psi_{2}\!\left(\frac{\widehat{t}_{i}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{2}}\right)\Big/\frac{\widehat{t}_{i}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{2}},\quad i=1,\dots,n\,,

(10)

with
\psi_{2}=\rho_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\widehat{t}_{i}
is
obtained from (
6
) with

\widehat{r}_{ij}
in place of
r_{ij}
.

By undoing the original
standardization by the diagonal matrix
\bm{\widehat{D}}
,
the cellCov estimates
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}
and
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}

of the overall
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{\Sigma}
in

d
dimensions are

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}:=\bm{\widehat{D}}\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\bm{z}^{k}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}:=\bm{\widehat{D}}\big(\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{k}}+\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}\big)\bm{\widehat{D}}\,.

(11)

The standardization step in the beginning ensures
that cellCov is scale equivariant, meaning that
for any diagonal matrix

\bm{A}=\operatorname{diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})

with
a_{j}>0
, the cellCov location and scatter
estimators of
\bm{\widetilde{Z}}\bm{A}
are
\bm{A}\bm{\widehat{\mu}}
and

\bm{A}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}\bm{A}
. Therefore,
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}
and

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
react in the usual way to changes of
variable units. We then define the cellMR
estimates of
\bm{B}
,
\bm{b}
, and
\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}
as
in (
2
)–(
3
).
We will discuss the tuning of the ridge
parameter
\lambda
later.

2.2
Out-of-sample prediction

When a new datapoint

\bm{x}_{*}=\left(x_{*1},\dots,x_{*p}\right)^{T}
arrives,
we wish to predict the unknown response
\bm{y}^{*}
.
If
\bm{x}_{*}
were clean, we could simply put

\bm{\widehat{y}}_{*}=\bm{\widehat{b}}+\bm{\widehat{B}}^{T}\bm{x}_{*}
. However, the task
becomes nontrivial since
\bm{x}_{*}
can also contain
NA’s and/or cellwise outliers. Fortunately
cellPCA can produce an imputed version of

\bm{x}_{*}
in which suspicious cells are cleaned and
missing cells are filled in, whereas the other
cells are kept as they were. The imputed point is
given by

x^{\mbox{\scriptsize{imp}}}_{*j}:=\widehat{x}_{*j}+w_{*j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}m_{*j}(x_{*j}-\widehat{x}_{*j})

(12)

where
\bm{\widehat{x}}_{*}=\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{X}\bm{u}^{X}_{*}+\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{X}
.
Here
\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{X}
and
\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{X}
are robust estimates
obtained by a separate cellPCA run on

\{\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}\}
. Also
the scores
\bm{u}^{X}_{*}
are provided by cellPCA, as
detailed in
Centofanti et al. 2026
.
The cellMR prediction
\bm{\widehat{y}}_{*}
of the response
of
\bm{x}_{*}
is then given by

\bm{\widehat{y}}_{*}:=\bm{\widehat{b}}+\bm{\widehat{B}}^{T}\bm{x}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{imp}}}_{*}.

2.3
Selecting tuning parameters

The cellMR method contains two knobs: the
dimension
k
of the principal subspace in
the PCA model (
4
), and the
parameter
\lambda
in the ridge
regularization (
2
).
Should the condition number of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x}

given by
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x})/\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{x})
be small,
say under
10^{5}
, we could just
put
\lambda=0
.

The most common method to select tuning
parameters in regression is cross-validation
(CV), see e.g.
Hastie et al. 2009
.

K
-fold CV randomly splits the dataset into

K
folds of size
n_{h}
for
h=1,\dots,K
.
For each
h
, the model is trained on the
union of the other
K-1
folds, and the
predictions are computed on fold
h
. The
resulting squared regression residuals are
then averaged to estimate the out-of-sample
mean squared error (MSE). The computation is
repeated on a grid of parameter values,
and the parameter with the smallest MSE
is selected.

We need to modify this approach for selecting
the couple
(k,\lambda)
because each
fold can contain outliers and NA’s. We will
estimate the prediction error by

\text{CV}(k,\lambda)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{h=1}^{K}\mbox{WMSE}_{(h)j}\;,

(13)

where
\mbox{WMSE}_{(h)j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}w_{(h)ij}(y_{(h)ij}-\widehat{y}_{(h)ij})^{2}/\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}w_{(h)ij}\big)

is a weighted mean of squared residuals in
fold
h
, that downweights cellwise and casewise
outliers. The weights are specified in
Supplementary Material
B
.
We then select the couple (
k,\lambda)

that minimizes (
13
).

2.4
Robustness properties

We now study the robustness properties of the cellMR
estimators
\bm{\widehat{b}}
and
\bm{\widehat{B}}
.
For this we will use the influence function (IF),
a standard robustness tool, which reveals how an
estimator changes as a function of the position of
the contamination.

Let
Z=(X^{T},Y^{T})^{T}
denote the
d
-variate random
variable obtained by concatenating the
p
-variate
predictor
X
and the
q
-variate response
Y

(so
d=p+q
), and let
H_{0}
be the distribution
of
Z
without contamination.
We now add both casewise and cellwise contamination.
Let
C\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a random vector with
unspecified outlier-generating distribution
H_{C}
.
The
mixed contamination model
says that
we observe

Z_{\varepsilon}=A\odot Z+(\bm{1}_{d}-A)\odot C,

(14)

where
A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\odot A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
. The casewise
contamination factor
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
has Bernoulli
marginals with
\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{j}=1)=1-\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}

for
j=1,\ldots,d
,
and its entries are fully dependent in the sense that

\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{1}=\cdots=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{d})=1
.
The cellwise contamination factor
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}

has independent entries
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}
for

j=1,\ldots,d
, that are Bernoulli random variables
with success probabilities

\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}=1)=1-\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}
.
Therefore the mixed model (
14
)
captures the simultaneous presence of casewise and
cellwise outliers in the regression setting.

For computing the IF we let the outlier distribution

H_{C}
be a point mass
\Delta_{\bm{c}}
, which is a
distribution that assigns all its mass to a point

\bm{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})^{T}
.
The classical casewise IF of
Hampel et al. 1986

replaces entire cases by outliers. It puts

A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
with
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
independent of
Z
.
In that situation, the distribution of

Z_{\varepsilon}
simplifies to
(1-\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}})H_{0}+\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\Delta_{\bm{c}}
. We denote the
distribution of
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
as
G_{\varepsilon}^{D}
and the
distribution of
Z_{\varepsilon}
as
H(G_{\varepsilon}^{D},\bm{c})
.
Writing an estimator as a functional
T
defined on
distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the casewise
influence function at the contamination point
\bm{c}

is defined as

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},T,H_{0}):=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon}T\bigl(H(G_{\varepsilon}^{D},\bm{c})\bigr)\right|_{\varepsilon=0}=\;\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{T\bigl(H(G_{\varepsilon}^{D},\bm{c})\bigr)-T(H_{0})}{\varepsilon}.

(15)

To capture the effect of outlying cells we adopt the
cellwise IF introduced by
Alqallaf et al. 2009
.
Now
A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
, where the entries
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}

are mutually independent and independent of
Z
.
The distribution of
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
is denoted as

G_{\varepsilon}^{I}
. The resulting distribution of

Z_{\varepsilon}
with
H_{C}=\Delta_{\bm{c}}
is denoted by

H(G_{\varepsilon}^{I},\bm{c})
. The cellwise influence function
of
T
at
\bm{c}
is then defined as

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},T,H_{0}):=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\varepsilon}T\bigl(H(G_{\varepsilon}^{I},\bm{c})\bigr)\right|_{\varepsilon=0}=\;\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{T\bigl(H(G_{\varepsilon}^{I},\bm{c})\bigr)-T(H_{0})}{\varepsilon}.

(16)

We denote the functionals corresponding to the
cellMR estimators
\bm{\widehat{b}}
and
\bm{\widehat{B}}
by

\bm{b}(H)
and
\bm{B}(H)
.
We will derive the IFs of
\bm{b}(H)
and of the

pq\times 1
column vector
\vect(\bm{B}(H))
,
where
\vect({\bm{\cdot}})
converts a matrix to a vector
by stacking its columns on top of each other.
The derivation of these IFs relies on the IFs of
the cellPCA and cellCov estimators and on the
various components that are used for their
construction, such as
\bm{\mu}_{z}(H)
,
\bm{V}(H)
,

\bm{\mu}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(H)
,

\bm{\Sigma}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(H)
, and
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}(H)
,
which are the functionals corresponding to

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}
and
\bm{\widehat{V}}
from (
5
),

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})
and

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})

in (
7
), and
\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}

in (
9
).

Proposition 1
.

The casewise and cellwise influence functions of

\vect(\bm{B})
and
\bm{b}
are

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{B}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{B}\Big[\bm{R}_{\Sigma,1}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bm{R}_{\Sigma,2}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{\Sigma}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}),H_{0}\right)+\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}),H_{0}\right)\Big],

(17)

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{b},H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{b,1}\Big[\bm{R}_{\mu}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bm{V}_{0}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}},H_{0}\right)+\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}_{z},H_{0}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;-\bm{R}_{b,2}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{B}),H_{0}\right),

(18)

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{B}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{B}\Big[\bm{R}_{\Sigma,1}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bm{R}_{\Sigma,2}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{\Sigma}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}),H_{0}\right)+\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}),H_{0}\right)\Big],

(19)

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{b},H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{b,1}\Big[\bm{R}_{\mu}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bm{V}_{0}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}},H_{0}\right)+\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}_{z},H_{0}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;-\bm{R}_{b,2}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\!\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{B}),H_{0}\right),

(20)

where
\bm{V}_{0}=\bm{V}(H_{0})
. The matrices
\bm{R}_{\Sigma,1}
,

\bm{R}_{\Sigma,2}
,
\bm{R}_{B}
,
\bm{R}_{b,1}
,
\bm{R}_{b,2}
,
and
\bm{R}_{\mu}
and the proofs are provided in
Supplementary Material
B
.

Let us look at a special case to get a feel for
these results. Consider i.i.d. data

z_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})^{T}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
with

z_{i}\sim N\!\left(\bm{0},\;\begin{bmatrix}1&0.9\\
0.9&1\end{bmatrix}\right)
. This obeys the simple linear model without
intercept
y_{i}=0.9\,x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}
with errors

\varepsilon_{i}\sim N(0,0.19)
. Figure
1

shows the casewise and cellwise IFs of the cellMR
slope. They are bounded, indicating that the estimator
is robust to both casewise and cellwise contamination.
The casewise IF (left) has some flat regions, where
moving an outlier further away makes no difference
because its weight is zero there. The cellwise IF
(right) is more smooth, with the influence largest
near the center and gradually decreasing as the
contamination becomes more distant. Together, these
plots illustrate that the estimator effectively
controls the influence of both casewise and cellwise
perturbations.

Figure 1:
The casewise (left) and cellwise (right)
IF of
\bm{B}
in a simple linear model.

3
Outlier detection

We construct numerical and graphical diagnostics to
gain further insight into outlying cells and cases
in the responses and the predictors. For each pair

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
we define the regression residual

\bm{r}_{i}:=\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\widehat{y}}_{i}
, where

\bm{\widehat{y}}_{i}=\bm{\widehat{b}}+\bm{\widehat{B}}^{T}\bm{x}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{imp}}}_{i}
with
\bm{\widehat{b}}
and
\bm{\widehat{B}}

denoting the cellMR estimates, and where
\bm{x}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{imp}}}_{i}
is
the imputed version of
\bm{x}_{i}
as in
Section
2.2
.

The outlier map of the regression shows the distances

\text{RD}_{i}=\sqrt{\bm{r}_{i}^{T}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\bm{r}_{i}}

of the regression residuals versus the distances

\text{PD}_{i}=\sqrt{(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{x})^{T}\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{x}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{x})}
of the predictors, where
\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{x}

and
\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{x}
are robust estimates of the
location and scatter of
\bm{x}_{i}
obtained as in

Centofanti et al. 2025
. Figure
2

displays the outlier map for a dataset with
p=50
,

q=3
and
n=59
that will be described in
Section
6
.

Figure 2:
A regression outlier map of cellMR.

The vertical dashed line is at the cutoff

c_{\mbox{\tiny{PD}}}=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{p,0.99}}
, while the
horizontal one shows the cutoff

c_{\mbox{\tiny{RD}}}=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{q,0.99}}
. Cases with
small
\text{RD}_{i}\leqslant c_{\mbox{\tiny{RD}}}
and small

\text{PD}_{i}\leqslant c_{\mbox{\tiny{PD}}}
are considered regular.
Cases with large
\text{PD}_{i}
and small

\text{RD}_{i}
are referred to as good leverage points.
Cases with large
\text{RD}_{i}
are considered vertical
outliers when
\text{PD}_{i}
is small, and bad leverage
points when
\text{PD}_{i}
is large.
Figure
2
contains two of the three
types of atypical observations.

The size of each point is made proportional to

1-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}m_{ij}w_{ij}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
.
A large point therefore indicates a case with many
outlying cells in the predictor and/or the response.
The casewise outlyingness is visualized by coloring
the points according to their casewise total deviation

t_{i}
of (
6
). The points are colored
black when
t_{i}>1.5\,c_{t,0.99}
, white when

t_{i}<c_{t,0.99}
, and use an interpolated
grayscale in between. Here the cutoff
c_{t,0.99}

is the
99
th percentile of the distribution of

t_{i}
simulated for uncontaminated data.

We can also visualize outlying cells by a cellmap

(
Rousseeuw and Van den Bossche 2018
)
. We construct the predictor
cellmap by computing the vector

\bm{r}_{i}^{X}=\bm{D}_{r^{X}}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{\widehat{x}}_{i})

for each
\bm{x}_{i}
where
\bm{\widehat{x}}_{i}
is the fitted
point
\bm{\widehat{x}}_{i}=\bm{\widetilde{V}}_{X}\bm{u}^{X}_{i}+\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{X}
as in
Section
2.2
, and
\bm{D}_{r^{X}}=\diag(\widehat{\sigma}^{r^{X}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\sigma}^{r^{X}}_{p})
with

\widehat{\sigma}^{r^{X}}_{j}=\sigma_{M}(\{x_{ij}-\widehat{x}_{ij}\}_{i=1}^{n})
.
These vectors are combined in an
n\times p

matrix, and visualized by coloring.
The left panel of Figure
3
shows
this map for the 4 cases labeled in
Figure
2
.

Figure 3:
cellMR predictor and residual cellmaps
of the 4 labeled cases.

In this map, cells with
|r_{ij}^{X}|\leqslant c_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}:=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{1,0.99}}
are considered
regular and colored yellow. Missing cells would be
white. The remaining cells are flagged as cellwise
outliers. Cells with
r_{ij}^{X}>c_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
range
from light orange to red, while cells with

r_{ij}^{X}<-c_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
are colored from light
purple to dark blue. We add information on casewise
outlyingness by drawing a circle to the right of
each row, using the same color scheme as in the
regression outlier map.

The right panel of Figure
3
is
the residual cellmap of the same cases. It
shows the standardized residual vectors

\bm{\widetilde{r}}_{i}=\bm{D}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{-1}(\bm{y}_{i}-\bm{\widehat{y}}_{i})
of
the regression, where
\bm{D}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}=\diag(\widehat{\sigma}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\sigma}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{q})

with
\widehat{\sigma}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\sigma}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{q}

the diagonal entries of
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}
. The
cells are colored according to the same scheme.

4
Robust inference via cellBoot

We want a confidence interval for a parameter of
interest
\theta_{0}
of the form

\theta_{0}=(\bm{b}^{T},\vect(\bm{B})^{T})\bm{a}
for some
fixed vector
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+pq}
. In
particular, each
b_{j}
and
B_{j\ell}
are of
this form. Let
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
denote the estimator
of
\theta_{0}
computed from the sample

\{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. From these data
the bootstrap draws a sample of size
n
with
replacement, denoted as

\{(\bm{x}_{i}^{*},\bm{y}_{i}^{*})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and used
to compute a bootstrap analogue
\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}

of
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
. Drawing many bootstrap samples
yields an empirical distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}

from which one constructs a confidence interval

C_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
for
\theta_{0}
with a
certain level
1-\alpha
. An important question
is whether the coverage probability of
C_{n}

is correct for
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Definition 2
.

A confidence interval
C_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
for

\theta_{0}
is asymptotically exact at
level
(1-\alpha)
if

\Pr\{C_{n}\;\mbox{{contains}}\;\theta_{0}\}\;\rightarrow\;1-\alpha\quad\text{ as }\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(21)

A necessary condition for (
21
)
is that
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
is a consistent estimator of

\theta_{0}
, that is
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\rightarrow_{p}\theta_{0}

where
\rightarrow_{p}
denotes convergence in
probability. Intuitively, if

\widehat{\theta}_{n}
would converge to a location different
from
\theta_{0}
, the coverage probability
would go down to zero as
C_{n}
shrinks for

n\rightarrow\infty
.

4.1
The Indirect Inference estimator

We would like to use the robust cellMR estimator

(\bm{\widehat{b}},\bm{\widehat{B}})
of Section
2
to
estimate
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
. However, this estimator is
not guaranteed to be consistent due to its
cellwise construction.

A solution comes from
indirect inference

(II), a simulation-based method that provides a
consistent estimator starting from an inconsistent
one
(
Gourieroux et al. 1993
;
Guerrier et al. 2019
)
.
This approach follows a two-step procedure: in the
first step, an auxiliary estimator is obtained
that may not be consistent; in the second step,
simulation-based bias correction is applied,
yielding a consistent estimator. Consistency of
the cellMR estimator relies on the consistency of
the estimators of the location
\bm{\mu}
and scatter

\bm{\Sigma}
of the joint distribution
F
of

(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})
. We will employ II to obtain
consistent estimators of
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{\Sigma}
.

Let
\{F_{\bm{\theta}}:\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}\}

denote a parametric family of distributions
indexed by the parameter

\bm{\theta}=(\bm{\mu}^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{\Sigma})^{T})^{T}
.
Here
\vecth_{s}(\bm{\Sigma})
denotes the scaled
half–vectorization, that first multiplies
the off-diagonal entries of
\bm{\Sigma}
by

\sqrt{2}
, and then stacks the lower
triangular entries (including the diagonal)
on top of each other. With this convention, the
Euclidean norm of
\vecth_{s}(\bm{\Sigma})
coincides
with the Frobenius norm of
\bm{\Sigma}
.
The parameter space
\bm{\Theta}
contains the
admissible parameters:
\bm{\Theta}=\Bigl\{\,(\bm{\mu}^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{\Sigma})^{T})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+d(d+1)/2}:\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{S}^{d},\ \|\bm{\mu}\|\leqslant M,c\leqslant\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Sigma})\leqslant\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Sigma})\leqslant C\Bigr\}

for
M<\infty
and
0<c\leqslant C<\infty
.
Here
\mathbb{S}^{d}
denotes the space of
symmetric
d\times d
matrices.
We assume that
F=F_{\bm{\theta}_{0}}
for some

\bm{\theta}_{0}\in\bm{\Theta}
.

Let
\widehat{\pi}_{n}(\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n})
denote the auxiliary estimator
of
\bm{\theta}_{0}
computed from the observed sample

\{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})\}_{i=n}^{n}
which depends on a
vector of tuning parameters

\bm{\eta}\in\bm{H}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
, where

\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n}
is an estimate of
\bm{\eta}
computed
from the observed sample. We further denote by

\widehat{\pi}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)
the auxiliary estimator
evaluated on a generic sample of size
n
generated
from
F_{\bm{\theta}}
, with
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, and
computed with the tuning parameters in
\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n}
.
The indirect estimator is then defined as

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}=\argzero_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\bigl\{\,\widehat{\pi}_{n}(\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n})-\overline{\pi}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)\bigr\},

(22)

where

\overline{\pi}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\widehat{\pi}_{h}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n),

(23)

and
\widehat{\pi}_{h}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)
denotes the
value of
\widehat{\pi}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)
computed on
the
h
-th simulated dataset of size
n
drawn from

F_{\bm{\theta}}
. The observations drawn from

F_{\bm{\theta}}
are generated using the same random
number generator seed for all
\bm{\theta}
to ensure
the objective function is deterministic.

Proposition 2
.

Under assumptions A1-A3 in Supplementary
Material
D
, any sequence

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}
satisfying

\bigl\|\widehat{\pi}_{n}(\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n})-\overline{\pi}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)\bigr\|\rightarrow_{p}0
is consistent for
\bm{\theta}_{0}
,
that is,
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}\rightarrow_{p}\bm{\theta}_{0}
.

The proof is presented in Section
D

of the Supplementary Material.

To obtain a solution of (
22
),
we iteratively update the estimate

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
as

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell)}\;=\;\Pi_{\bm{\Theta}}\!\left(\widehat{\pi}_{n}(\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n})+\Big[\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell-1)}-\overline{\pi}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell-1)},\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n},n)\Big]\right),

(24)

starting from
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(0)}=\widehat{\pi}_{n}(\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n})
.
Here the function
\Pi_{\bm{\Theta}}
brings its
argument into
\bm{\Theta}
if it wasn’t already in it.
Starting from a vector
(\bm{a}^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{L})^{T})^{T}

in
\mathbb{R}^{d+d(d+1)/2}
,

\Pi_{\bm{\Theta}}(\bm{a}^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{L})^{T})^{T}:=\bigl(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{T},\ \vecth_{s}(\tilde{\bm{L}}))^{T}\bigr)^{T}
,
where
\tilde{\bm{a}}
is equal to
\bm{a}
if

\|\bm{a}\|\leqslant M
, and

\displaystyle M(\bm{a}/\|\bm{a}\|)
otherwise. This
clips
\bm{a}
to the ball of radius
M
. Also,
\bm{L}

is decomposed as
\bm{L}=\bm{Q}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{Q}^{T}
with
\bm{\Lambda}=\diag(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})
, and
turned into
\tilde{\bm{L}}=\bm{Q}\bm{\Lambda}_{c}\bm{Q}^{T}
where

\bm{\Lambda}_{c}=\diag(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}_{d})
, with

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}=\min\{\max\{\lambda_{j},c\},C\}
.
This clips the eigenvalues of
\bm{L}
to the
interval
[c,C]
, leaving eigenvectors unchanged.

Under appropriate conditions, the limit of the
sequence
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
for

\ell\rightarrow\infty
indeed exists and is
the unique solution
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}

of (
22
), as shown in the
following proposition.

Proposition 3
.

Under assumptions B1-B2 in Supplementary
Material
D
, we have that
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}
is
unique and the sequence
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
converges
in norm to
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}
with linear rate for every

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(0)}\in\bm{\Theta}
, that is,

\|\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(\ell)}-\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}\|\leqslant L^{\ell}\|\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{(0)}-\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}\|
for some
L<1
.

4.2
The auxiliary FastCellCov estimator

The robust cellMR regression is derived
directly from the cellCov estimates
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}

and
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}
through formula (
2
).
In our setting, where the data may be
contaminated by cellwise and casewise outliers,
we would like to use cellCov as our auxiliary
estimator. However, in the II algorithm the
auxiliary estimator has to be recomputed many
times, and using cellCov would be too expensive
computationally. This motivates the construction
of the FastCellCov estimator, which preserves
the main robustness ideas of cellCov but avoids
repeating the most expensive parts of the
algorithm.

FastCellCov starts by applying cellCov to the
original sample
\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{n}
. Now
consider another sample

\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{1}^{*},\dots,\bm{\widetilde{z}}_{n}^{*}
, that may be
a bootstrap sample or a simulated sample of
size
n
generated from
F_{\bm{\theta}}
. We then
standardize it by computing

\bm{z}_{i}^{*}=\bm{\widehat{D}}^{-1}\bm{\widetilde{z}}^{*}_{i}
where
\bm{\widehat{D}}
is
the diagonal matrix of scale estimators of the
original sample, as in
Section
2.1
.
Then, FastCellCov computes a robust center

\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{F}
and a robust covariance matrix

\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{F}
of the
\bm{z}_{i}^{*}
. These quantities
are computed as weighted versions of the sample
mean and covariance matrix. The weights are
designed to downweight both cellwise and
casewise outliers, while also accounting for
the possible presence of missing values, and a
reconstructed by reusing the robust structure
learned from the original sample. This
construction follows the same principles as
the Detecting Deviating Cells (DDC) method of

Rousseeuw and Van den Bossche 2018
where each cell is compared
with a robust prediction obtained from the
variables that are sufficiently correlated
with it, and cells that are incompatible with
this prediction receive a smaller weight. Thus,
FastCellCov should be viewed as a one-step
approximation to
cellCov
. The entire
reasoning can be found in
Supplementary Material
C
.
The final FastCellCov estimates of
\bm{\mu}

and
\bm{\Sigma}
are then given by

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{F}:=\bm{\widehat{D}}\bm{\widetilde{\mu}}_{F}
and

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{F}:=\bm{\widehat{D}}\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{F}\bm{\widehat{D}}
.

4.3
The cellBoot algorithm

The cellBoot algorithm consists of the following
steps:

Step 1.

Apply the
cellMR
estimator to the
original dataset
\{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}

to obtain estimates of
\bm{\mu}
,
\bm{\Sigma}
, and
the tuning parameters
k
and
\lambda
.

Step 2.

Generate bootstrap samples

\{(\bm{x}^{*}_{bi},\bm{y}^{*}_{bi})\}_{i=1}^{n}
from the
data, for
b=1,\dots,B
.

Step 3.

For each

\{(\bm{x}^{*}_{bi},\bm{y}^{*}_{bi})\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
compute the FastcellCov estimates
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{F,b}

and
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{F,b}
as in

Section
4.2
. Then
compute the corrected versions

\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{II,b}^{*}
and

\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{II,b}^{*}
by applying II as in
Section
4.1
. Next, compute the
estimates
(\bm{\widehat{b}}^{*}_{b},\bm{\widehat{B}}^{*}_{b})
of the
coefficient matrix
\bm{B}
and intercept
\bm{b}

according to (
2
).

Step 4.

Construct the confidence interval for the
parameter of interest at level
1-\alpha
by
computing the
\alpha/2
and
1-\alpha/2

quantiles of the bootstrap estimates

\{\widehat{\theta}^{*}_{b}\}_{b=1}^{B}
with
\widehat{\theta}^{*}_{b}=((\bm{\widehat{b}}_{b}^{*})^{T},\vect(\bm{\widehat{B}}_{b}^{*})^{T})\bm{a}
.

The number of bootstrap replications
B
determines
the accuracy: higher
B
values yield more stable
confidence intervals at the cost of increased
computation. In our implementation we set

B=1000
, which offers a suitable trade-off
between precision and computation time.

The following theorem establishes the
consistency of the bootstrap distribution of

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{*}-\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n})
for
approximating the sampling distribution of

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}-\bm{\theta}_{0})
. Here

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}=\big((\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{II})^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{II})^{T}\big)^{T}
denotes the
II estimator using FastcellCov as auxiliary
estimator,
applied
to the observed sample,
and
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{*}=\big((\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{II}^{*})^{T},\vecth_{s}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{II}^{*})^{T}\big)^{T}

is its bootstrap counterpart.

Theorem 1
.

Under assumptions D1–D7 in Section
D

of the Supplementary Material, the bootstrap
distribution of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{*}-\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n})

is consistent for the distribution of

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}-\bm{\theta}_{0})
in
Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance, that is,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d+d(d+1)/2}}\Big|\Pr\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}-\bm{\theta}_{0})\leqslant x\bigr)-{\Pr}^{*}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{*}-\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n})\leqslant x\,\bigr)\Big|\rightarrow_{p}0,

where
{\Pr}^{*}
is computed under the bootstrap
distribution, conditional on the observed data.

Define the scalar parameters

\widehat{\theta}_{n}=\bigl(\bm{\widehat{b}}^{T},\vect(\bm{\widehat{B}})^{T}\bigr)\bm{a}

and
\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}=\bigl((\bm{\widehat{b}}^{*})^{T},\vect(\bm{\widehat{B}}^{*})^{T}\bigr)\bm{a}
,
where
(\bm{\widehat{b}},\bm{\widehat{B}})
and
(\bm{\widehat{b}}^{*},\bm{\widehat{B}}^{*})

are obtained from
\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}
and

\bm{\widehat{\theta}}_{n}^{*}
through (
2
).
Then Theorem
1
applies
directly to the distributions of

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})
and

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})
:

Corollary 1
.

Under assumptions D1-D8 in Supplementary
Material
D
, it holds that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\Pr\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})\leqslant x\bigr)-{\Pr}^{*}\bigl(\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{*}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})\leqslant x\bigr)\Big|\rightarrow_{p}0.

The next corollary of
Theorem
1
guarantees
that the proposed bootstrap confidence interval
for
\theta_{0}
is asymptotically exact at level

(1-\alpha)
.

Corollary 2
.

Under assumptions D1–D8 in Supplementary
Material
D
it holds for all

\alpha\in(0,1)
that

\widehat{C}_{n}:=\bigl[\hat{c}_{n}^{*}(\alpha/2),\ \hat{c}_{n}^{*}(1-\alpha/2)\bigr]
is asymptotically
exact at level
1-\alpha
, where

\hat{c}_{n}^{*}(\alpha/2)
and

\hat{c}_{n}^{*}(1-\alpha/2)
are the
\alpha/2

and
1-\alpha/2
empirical quantiles
of the distribution of
\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{\,*}
.

4.4
Robustness properties

We now study the influence of data contamination
on the cellBoot confidence interval
\widehat{C}_{n}
for

\theta_{0}
. We will use the contamination model
of Section
2.4
,
with
H_{0}:=F_{\bm{\theta}_{0}}
.
The center of
\widehat{C}_{n}
is
m_{n}:=(\hat{c}^{*}(\alpha/2)+\hat{c}^{*}(1-\alpha/2))/2
,
and its length is
\ell_{n,\alpha}=\hat{c}^{*}(1-\alpha/2)-\hat{c}^{*}(\alpha/2)
.
Since the bootstrap estimator admits the
decomposition

\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{\,*}=\widehat{\theta}_{n}+n^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{\,*}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})
, a
bootstrap
\gamma
-quantile can be written

\hat{c}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)=\widehat{\theta}_{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\hat{q}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)
, where
\hat{q}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)

denotes the
\gamma
-quantile of the conditional
distribution of

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}^{\,*}-\widehat{\theta}_{n})
given the
observed sample. The center and the length of
the interval can thus be written as

m_{n}=\widehat{\theta}_{n}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\bigl\{\hat{q}^{*}(\alpha/2)+\hat{q}^{*}(1-\alpha/2)\bigr\}
,
and
\ell_{n,\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigl\{\hat{q}_{n}^{*}(1-\alpha/2)-\hat{q}_{n}^{*}(\alpha/2)\bigr\}
.

Under
H_{0}
, Theorem
1

implies that the center
m_{n}
and the scaled
length
\sqrt{n}\,\ell_{n,\alpha}
converge in
probability to
\theta_{0}
and

q(1-\alpha/2)-q(\alpha/2)
, where

q(\alpha/2)
and
q(1-\alpha/2)
are the

\alpha/2
and
(1-\alpha/2)
quantiles of the
asymptotic distribution of

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0})
.

At a generic distribution
H
we define the center
functional as
m(H)=T(H)
where
T(H)
is the
functional version of the estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{n}

under
H
. We define the scaled length functional as

\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha}(H)=Q_{H}(1-\alpha/2)-Q_{H}(\alpha/2)
,
where
Q_{H}(\gamma)
denotes the
\gamma
-quantile
of the asymptotic distribution of

\sqrt{n}\{T(\widehat{H}_{n})-T(H)\}
, where

\widehat{H}_{n}
is the empirical distribution
obtained from an i.i.d. sample of size
n
drawn
from
H
.

Proposition 4
.

Under assumptions E1–E2 in Supplementary
Material
E
it holds
that

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},m,H_{0})=\bm{m}_{c}^{T}Z_{F}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta}_{0}),

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},m,H_{0})=d\,\bm{m}_{c}^{T}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\E_{H(j,\bm{c})}[Z_{F}(X,\bm{\eta}_{0})]\right),

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha},H_{0})=2z_{1-\alpha/2}\Big[m_{s,1}

\displaystyle+\bm{m}_{s,2}^{T}\,\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta},H_{0})

\displaystyle+\bm{m}_{s,3}^{T}Z_{F}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta}_{0})+\bm{m}_{s,4}^{T}\!\bigl(Z_{F}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta}_{0})\otimes Z_{F}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta}_{0})\bigr)\Big],

\displaystyle\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha},H_{0})=2z_{1-\alpha/2}\Big[dm_{s,1}+

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{s,2}^{T}\,\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta},H_{0})+d\,\bm{m}_{s,3}^{T}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\E_{H(j,\bm{c})}\!\big[Z_{F}(X,\bm{\eta}_{0})\big]\right)

\displaystyle+d\,\bm{m}_{s,4}^{T}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\E_{H(j,\bm{c})}\!\bigl[Z_{F}(X,\bm{\eta}_{0})\otimes Z_{F}(X,\bm{\eta}_{0})\bigr]\right)\Big].

Here
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product,

\bm{\eta}_{0}:=\bm{\eta}(H_{0})
with
\bm{\eta}(\cdot)
the
functional corresponding to the tuning parameter
estimator
\bm{\widehat{\eta}}_{n}
in the auxiliary estimator,

H(j,\bm{c})
is the distribution of
X\sim H_{0}

but with its
j
-th component fixed at the
constant
c_{j}
, and
z_{\gamma}
denotes the

\gamma
-quantile of the standard normal
distribution. The quantities
\bm{m}_{c}
,
m_{s,1}
,

\bm{m}_{s,2}
,
\bm{m}_{s,3}
,
\bm{m}_{s,4}
and
the function
Z_{F}
are defined in Supplementary
Material
E
where
these results are proved, and

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta},H_{0})

and
\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\eta},H_{0})

are the casewise and cellwise IFs of
\bm{\eta}
.

Figure 4:
The casewise (left) and cellwise (right)
influence function of
m
and
\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha}

for the proposed bootstrap percentile confidence
interval of the slope with
1-\alpha=0.9
.

To gain intuition for these results, we
consider the bivariate model introduced in
Section
2.4
.
Figure
4
shows the casewise and
cellwise IF of the center
m
and the scaled
length
\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha}
of the
1-\alpha=0.9

confidence interval of the slope. Their
shape is involved due to the complex
construction of the cellBoot intervals.
The main feature is that all four IFs are
bounded, indicating that
m
and

\widetilde{\ell}_{\alpha}
are robust against casewise
and cellwise contamination.

5
Simulation study

We assess the performance of cellMR and cellBoot
through a Monte Carlo experiment, where the
clean data are generated according to the linear
model (
1
). The predictors follow
a multivariate normal distribution with

\bm{\mu}=\bm{0}
and covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{x}
with
entries
\sigma_{x,j\ell}=(-0.4)^{|j-\ell|}
.
The coefficient matrix
\bm{B}
has entries drawn
from
N(0,0.04)
. To control the
signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), the error
covariance is
\bm{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}=\bm{I}_{q}(\Tr(\bm{B}^{T}\bm{\Sigma}_{x}\bm{B})/q)/\text{SNR}
.
We fix
\text{SNR}=10
.

Three contamination scenarios are considered,
with outlier fraction
\varepsilon=0.2
.
In the cellwise outlier scenario, we replace
a fraction
\varepsilon
of random entries
x_{ij}

and
y_{ij}
by
\gamma\sigma_{jj}
, where

\sigma_{j\ell}
are the entries of the

(p+q)\times(p+q)
covariance matrix

\bm{\Sigma}
. The contamination position
\gamma

varies from
1
to
7
. In the casewise
outlier scenario, a fraction
\varepsilon
of the

(\bm{x}_{i}^{T},\bm{y}_{i}^{T})^{T}

are generated from
N(0.2\gamma d\sqrt{d}\,\bm{e}/\sqrt{\bm{e}^{T}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{e}}\,,\bm{\Sigma})

where
d=p+q
and
\bm{e}
is the eigenvector
of
\bm{\Sigma}

with smallest eigenvalue. Finally, in the
mixed contamination scenario, the data
contains a fraction
\varepsilon/2
of cellwise
outliers and a fraction
\varepsilon/2
of casewise
outliers. We label the clean data by

\gamma=0
.

5.1
Predictive performance of cellMR

We measure predictive performance on a clean
test set
(\bm{x}_{test,i},\bm{y}_{test,i})
of size

\widetilde{n}=1000
by the mean squared error

\mbox{MSE}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{n}}\|\bm{y}_{test,i}-\bm{\widehat{b}}-\bm{\widehat{B}}^{T}\bm{x}_{test,i}\|^{2}
.
In each simulation setting we generate 200
datasets and report the average MSE.
Each dataset is composed of
n=100

observations with
p=q=\{10,25,50\}
.
The competing approaches include the classical
RIDGE regression, the CRM estimator of

Filzmoser et al. 2020
implemented in the

R
package
crmReg
, the REGCELL
method of
Su et al. 2024
implemented in the

R
package
regcell
, the
multivariate S-estimator (SEST) of

Van Aelst and Willems 2005
implemented in the

R
package
FRB
, the robust
penalized ridge version of the adaptive elastic
net proposed by
Cohen Freue et al. 2019
,
denoted as PENSE and implemented in the

R
package
pense
, and the sparse
robust regression method of

Bottmer et al. 2022
referred to as SHOOT.
Methods that were not explicitly designed for
multivariate regression are carried out by
fitting
q
separate regression models, one for
each response variable. The cellMR tuning
parameters
k
and
\lambda
are selected as
described in Section
2.3
using

10
-fold cross-validation.

Cellwise

Casewise

Casewise & Cellwise

p=q=10

p=q=25

p=q=50

Figure 5:
Average MSE attained by RIDGE, SEST,
PENSE, CRM, REGCELL, SHOOT, and cellMR in the
presence of cellwise outliers, casewise
outliers, or both, without missing data.

Figure
5
shows
the results, without missing data.
In the top row we see the results for the
low-dimensional setting with
p=q=10
.
The nonrobust RIDGE estimator gets a high MSE
in all three contamination settings, where
also REGCELL and SHOOT perform poorly. SEST,
PENSE, and CRM perform well under casewise
contamination, but not when cellwise outliers
are present. In contrast, cellMR performs well
in all three scenarios. The middle row of
Figure
5

displays qualitatively similar results for

p=q=25
.

In the bottom row
p=q=50
, so

n=p+q
. In this high-dimensional setting
SEST and CRM did not yield results, and SHOOT
gave a bad fit. Overall, we conclude that
cellMR is the only method that achieves
satisfactory performance across all settings.
Very similar results are obtained when setting

10\%
of randomly selected cells in both the
predictors and responses to NA, as seen in
Figure
11
in
Supplementary Material
F
.

5.2
Inference performance of cellBoot

To evaluate the inference performance of cellBoot
we compute the empirical coverage probability as
the proportion of Monte Carlo replications where
the true parameter value lies inside its
confidence interval. In each setting we generate
200 datasets of
n=400
observations with

p=q=\{10,30,60\}
and compute the confidence
intervals with nominal level of 90% of all
entries of
\bm{B}
. Ideally, the empirical
coverage should be close to 90%.

We compare cellBoot with the classical bootstrap
percentile method applied to the OLS estimator,
and with the Fast and Robust Bootstrap (FRB)
method of
Van Aelst and Willems 2005
. In the
higher-dimensional settings, the OLS-based
procedure uses the generalized inverse of the
sample predictor covariance matrix.

Cellwise

Casewise

Casewise & Cellwise

p=q=10

p=q=30

p=q=60

Figure 6:
Coverage attained by OLS,
FRB, and cellBoot for the
0.9
-level confidence intervals of the regression coefficients in the presence
of cellwise outliers, casewise outliers, or
both without missing data.

Figure
6

shows the resulting coverages. In the
uncontaminated setting (
\gamma=0
), cellBoot
attains the nominal
0.9
coverage level,
corroborating the theoretical results established
in Section
4
. As expected, OLS and
FRB attain the desired level as well. But under
contamination, the differences become substantial.
OLS exhibits severe undercoverage in all contaminated
scenarios. FRB remains reliable in the low-dimensional
setting (
p=q=10
) under casewise contamination, but its
performance deteriorates rapidly as the dimensions
increase. At the high dimensions
p=q=60
, FRB crashed.
In contrast, cellBoot maintains its coverage close to
the nominal level for large
\gamma
, in all settings.
This is because far away cells or cases receive a
small weight, so they do not have much effect on
the inference.

We also repeated this simulation when
setting
10\%
of the cells to NA in the
predictors and the responses.
The results are very similar, and shown in
Supplementary Material
F
.

6
Real data example

To illustrate the proposed regression and inference
procedure we use a well-known genomic dataset from

Shankavaram et al. 2007
that was also analyzed
by
Alfons et al. 2013
. It contains protein
and gene expression measurements for
n=59
human cancer
cell lines. Our goal is to investigate the relationship
between the expression levels of
p=50
genes and those
of
q=3
proteins, called MLH1, PRKCI, and RELA.
The set of genes was chosen as those most strongly
correlated with the protein responses. The aim is to
assess how well gene expression information can predict
protein expression levels, as proteins are the main
drivers of cellular behavior and are frequently
dysregulated in cancer.
We compare the performance of cellMR with the
competing methods RIDGE, REGCELL, and PENSE,
presented in Section
5.1
. In this
setting where
n\approx p
, SEST and CRM
did not work and SHOOT gave a bad fit. To evaluate
prediction performance while accounting for potential
outliers, we sort the squared regression residuals

r_{ij}^{2}=(y_{ij}-\widehat{y}_{ij})^{2}
to

r_{(1)j}^{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant r_{(n)j}^{2}

and define the robust trimmed Root Mean Squared
Error (trimRMSE) as

\mathrm{trimRMSE}_{\alpha}=\left(\frac{1}{qH_{\alpha}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{i=1}^{H_{\alpha}}r_{(i)j}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

where
H_{\alpha}=\lceil\alpha n\rceil
and

0<\alpha<1
denotes the trimming level.
To avoid an optimistic bias in the performance
assessment, the residuals

r_{ij}=y_{ij}-\widehat{y}_{ij}
are computed by
10-fold CV.

Figure 7:
\mathrm{trimRMSE}_{\alpha}
of
RIDGE, PENSE, REGCELL, and cellMR in function
of
\alpha\in[0.5,1]
.

Figure
7
shows the

\mathrm{trimRMSE}_{\alpha}
attained by RIDGE,
PENSE, REGCELL, and cellMR as a function of

\alpha\in[0.5,1]
. SHOOT is not shown as it
had very poor predictive performance here.
CellMR achieves the best predictive performance
for
\alpha\leqslant 0.85
where trimming
excluded the largest residuals.
For
\alpha
over 85% the curve
shoots up, indicating about 15% of poorly
fitted responses that might contain outliers.

To better understand these results,
we look at the regression outlier map in
Figure
2
in
Section
3
. Case 51 has
a large residual. There are several
leverage points, with predictor distances
exceeding the vertical cutoff.
Case 20 is a good leverage point, and 5
is a bad one. The cases with several
flagged outlying cells are shown
as points with larger sizes. The casewise
outliers are plotted as dark grey and black
points.

Figure
3
displays the
predictor and residual cellmaps of cases 5,
20, 38, and 51. Case 5 is flagged as a
casewise outlier, as indicated by its dark
circles. Case 20 is a good leverage point,
with typical residuals but several outlying
cells in the predictors. Case 38 seems clean.
We also see that case 51 has outlying negative
residuals in the variables MLH1 and PRKCI.

To further investigate the relationship between
genes and proteins, Figure
8

displays a forest plot. For each protein, it shows
the cellMR regression coefficients and their 95%
cellBoot confidence intervals for a selected set
of genes. The plot facilitates comparisons between
gene coefficients and their uncertainties.
We see that the effects of all selected genes on
PRKCI are deemed nonzero, indicating a stable
association between these genes and the protein
PRKCI. In contrast, for predicting MLH1 only
fewer genes have confidence intervals excluding
zero, suggesting a weaker association (based on
this dataset with low
n
). The predictive
strength for RELA is similar. Moreover, the
relatively narrow bootstrap intervals of several
coefficients indicate that cellBoot provides stable
inference despite the presence of contamination.

Figure 8:
cellMR forest plot with level 0.95 bootstrap
confidence intervals for the gene-protein data.

7
Conclusions

We have introduced the cellwise multivariate regression
(cellMR) estimator, a novel robust regression method
capable of simultaneously handling cellwise outliers,
casewise outliers, missing data, and high dimensions.
The method builds upon a recent robust covariance
estimator and integrates it within a multivariate linear
regression structure with ridge regularization. To the
best of our knowledge, this is the first multivariate
regression approach that addresses all these
challenges, and it does so with a unified methodology.
We also constructed visualizations of both cellwise
and casewise outliers, facilitating
anomaly detection and interpretation.

We complemented cellMR with cellBoot, a new
bootstrap-based inference procedure. It leverages
indirect inference to construct a consistent estimator
of the sampling distribution. This procedure
provides valid confidence intervals that remain stable
under the possibly simultaneous presence of cellwise
and casewise contamination and missing values. We think
that cellBoot is the first inference framework
specifically designed for this setting.

We established several theoretical properties of the
proposed methodology. We derived the influence
functions of the cellMR regression estimators,
and we were able to prove the asymptotic consistency
of cellBoot. We also derived the influence functions of
the center and the length of the resulting confidence
intervals.

The excellent finite-sample performance of cellMR and
cellBoot was verified through extensive
simulation, confirming their robustness and stability
across a wide range of contamination scenarios and
dimensions. A real data application from genomics
illustrated the practical utility of the proposed
approach.

Future research may extend the cellMR framework in
several directions, including extensions to
generalized linear models and adaptations to
structured high-dimensional settings such as graphical
or functional regression models. Moreover, the
cellBoot principle could serve as a general template
for robust inference in the presence of cellwise
outliers.

Software availability.
A zip file with
the R code, an example script, and the dataset is at

https://wis.kuleuven.be/statdatascience/code/cellmr_r_code.zip

abc

Supplementary Material to:
Cellwise and Casewise Robust

Multivariate Regression with Inference

Appendix A
More on M-estimation

The hyperbolic
tangent (
tanh
) function
\rho_{b,c}

introduced by
Hampel et al. 1981

is defined piecewise by

\rho_{b,c}(z)=\begin{cases}z^{2}/2&\mbox{ if }0\leqslant|z|\leqslant b,\\
d-(q_{1}/q_{2})\ln(\cosh(q_{2}(c-|z|)))&\mbox{ if }b\leqslant|z|\leqslant c,\\
d&\mbox{ if }c\leqslant|z|,\\
\end{cases}

(A.1)

where
d=(b^{2}/2)+(q_{1}/q_{2})\ln(\cosh(q_{2}(c-b)))
.
Its first derivative
\psi_{b,c}=\rho^{\prime}_{b,c}
has
been used as the
wrapping function

(
Raymaekers and Rousseeuw 2021
)
and equals

\psi_{b,c}(z)=\begin{cases}z&\mbox{ if }0\leqslant|z|\leqslant b,\\
q_{1}\tanh(q_{2}(c-|z|))\,\mbox{sign}(z)&\mbox{ if }b\leqslant|z|\leqslant c,\\
0&\mbox{ if }c\leqslant|z|\,.\\
\end{cases}

(A.2)

The function
\psi_{b,c}
is continuous, which
implies certain constraints on
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
.
CellMR uses the default wrapping
function shown in Figure
9
,
which has
b=1.5
and
c=4
with

q_{1}=1.54
and
q_{2}=0.86
.

Figure 9:
The function
\rho_{b,c}
with

b=1.5
and
c=4
(top left), its derivative

\psi_{b,c}
(top right), its
weight function
used in (
8
)
and (
10
) (bottom left), and
the function
z\mapsto\rho(\sqrt{z})
(bottom right).

The bottom left panel of
Figure
9

shows the weight function
w(z)
used
in (
8
)
and (
10
).

Proposition 5
.

The hyperbolic tangent function
\rho_{b,c}
in
(
A.1
) is a valid
\rho
-function.

The proof is given in
Centofanti et al. 2026
. The
bottom right panel of
Figure
9
illustrates the
concavity of the function

z\mapsto\rho_{b,c}(\sqrt{z})
.
The choice of the hyperbolic tangent

\rho
-function is further motivated by both
theoretical and practical considerations.
First note that, since
\rho_{b,c}
is
constant outside of
[-c,c]
, its derivative

\psi_{b,c}(z)
and the corresponding weight
function are zero for large positive and
negative values of
z
. Such functions are
said to be redescending in the sense that
very extreme values receive zero weight
in the estimation. This favorable property
is not shared by the well-known Huber

\rho
-function
(
Huber 1964
)
,
that is not suitable in our framework.
Moreover,
Hampel et al. 1986
show that the
function
\rho_{b,c}
arises as the unique
solution to the V-robustness problem. This
problem seeks to maximize the estimator’s
efficiency subject to an upper bound on its
change-of-variance function, that measures
how much the asymptotic variance changes
under point contamination.
The optimization is performed over all
bounded redescending
\rho
-functions.
Moreover, the tanh
\rho
-function
possesses several other desirable robustness
properties, including qualitative robustness,
low gross-error sensitivity, and a maximal
breakdown value. For a comparison between
redescending
\rho
-functions see Table 3
in Section 2.6 of
Hampel et al. 1986
.
From a practical point of view, another
advantage of
\psi_{b,c}
is that it is
linear in the central region
[-b,b]
. This
makes the weight exactly 1 in that
region, so inlying cells will not be
downweighted at all. That is an advantage
over other valid
\rho
-functions
that could have been used, such as
Tukey’s biweight
\rho
-function.

An M-scale
\sigma_{M}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
of a
univariate sample
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
is
defined as the solution
\hat{\sigma}>0
of
the estimating equation

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi\!\left(\frac{z_{i}}{\sigma}\right)=0.

(A.3)

We employ a redescending M-scale whose
associated
\chi
-function is given by

\chi_{b,c}(x)=\begin{cases}x^{2}-1+a,&|x|\leqslant b,\\[6.0pt]
\sqrt{A(k-1)}\;\tanh\!\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{(k-1)B^{2}}{A}}\bigl(\log(c)-\log|x|\bigr)\right),&b\leqslant|x|\leqslant c,\\[10.0pt]
0,&|x|\geqslant c,\end{cases}

where the constant
a
is chosen to ensure continuity at
|x|=b
, namely

a=\sqrt{A(k-1)}\,\tanh\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{(k-1)B^{2}}{A}}\log\!\left(\frac{c}{b}\right)\right)-(b^{2}-1)
.
The parameters
A
,
B
, and
k
are determined
according to the optimal V-robust redescending
construction described in Section 2.6 of

Hampel et al. 1986
. In the implementation of

\sigma_{M}
, we use
\chi_{b,c}
with
b=1.5
and

c=4
. The resulting redescending function is
displayed in Figure
10
. The
function
\chi
is bounded and redescends to
zero for
|x|>c
, so extreme observations
receive zero weight. In the simulations, the
reduced bias proved to be very helpful for
the resulting inference.

Figure 10:
The function
\chi_{b,c}
with

b=1.5
and
c=4
.

Appendix B
More on cellMR regression

We first address the selection of the couple

(k,\lambda)
by cross-validation, where
the folds can contain outliers and NA’s.
Section
2.3
estimates the
prediction error by formula (
13
):

\text{CV}(k,\lambda)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{h=1}^{K}\mbox{WMSE}_{(h)j}\;,

where
\mbox{WMSE}_{(h)j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}w_{(h)ij}(y_{(h)ij}-\widehat{y}_{(h)ij})^{2}/\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}w_{(h)ij}\big)

is a weighted mean of the squared residuals in
fold
h
. The weights in this formula are given by

w_{(h)ij}=m_{(h)ij}^{y}w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}},y}_{(h)i}(w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}},y}_{(h)ij})^{2}\;.

Here
m_{(h)ij}^{y}
is the missingness indicator
of
y_{(h)ij}
, so missing responses are of
course excluded. We also downweight outlying
cells, by

w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}},y}_{(h)ij}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\frac{y_{(h)ij}-\widehat{y}_{(h)ij}}{\widehat{\sigma}_{1,j}^{y}}\right)

where
w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
is the univariate function in the
bottom left panel of Figure
9
, and

\widehat{\sigma}_{(h)1,j}^{y}:=\sigma_{M}(\{y_{(h)ij}-\widehat{y}_{(h)ij}\}_{i=1}^{n})
.

The weight
w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{(h)i}
is given by

w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}},y}_{(h)i}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(d_{(h)i}/\widehat{\sigma}_{(h)2}^{y}\right)
where
w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}

is a function of the same type,

d_{(h)i}:=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{q}m_{(h)ij}^{y}w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}},y}_{(h)ij}}\sum_{j=1}^{q}m_{(h)ij}^{y}w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}},y}_{(h)ij}(y_{(h)ij}-\widehat{y}_{(h)ij})^{2}\,,

and
\widehat{\sigma}_{(h)2}^{y}:=\sigma_{M}(\{d_{(h)i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
.
We then select the couple (
k,\lambda)

that minimizes (
13
).

We now turn to the proofs of the influence functions
of cellMR. We consider the contamination
model (
14
) with
H_{C}=\Delta_{\bm{c}}
,
where
\Delta_{\bm{c}}
denotes the distribution that
assigns unit mass to a vector
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})^{T}
,
thus

Z_{\varepsilon}=A\odot Z+(\bm{1}_{d}-A)\odot\bm{c},

(A.4)

where
Z\sim H_{0}
and
A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\odot A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
.
We denote the distribution of
Z_{\varepsilon}
as
H_{\varepsilon}
,
and the distribution of
A
as
G_{\varepsilon}
.
The casewise contamination component
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
has Bernoulli marginals with

\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{j}=1)=1-\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
, and its components are fully dependent, in the sense that

\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{1}=\cdots=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}_{d})=1
.
The cellwise contamination component
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}

has components
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,d
, that are Bernoulli random variables with success probabilities

\Pr(A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}=1)=1-\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}
.

Under the
fully dependent contamination
model
(FDCM) we have

A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
with independent
Z
and

A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}
. We denote
G_{\varepsilon}
as

G_{\varepsilon}^{D}
and the distribution
H_{\varepsilon}
of
Z_{\varepsilon}
as

H(G_{\varepsilon}^{D},\bm{c})
.
In the
fully independent
contamination model
(FICM),
G_{\varepsilon}
is denoted by
G_{\varepsilon}^{I}
and the corresponding contaminated distribution
H_{\varepsilon}
by
H(G_{\varepsilon}^{I},\bm{c})
.
In this setting
A=A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
, where the components
A^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}
are mutually independent and independent of
Z
.

Under both the dependent and independent
contamination models,
the distribution of
A
satisfies

\Pr\left(A_{j}=1\right)=1-\varepsilon
,
j=1,\dots,d
,
and (ii) for any sequence

\left(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{d}\right)
of zeroes
and ones with
d-\ell
ones and
\ell
zeroes,

\Pr(A_{1}=j_{1},\ldots,A_{d}=j_{d})
has the same
value, denoted as
\delta_{\ell}(\varepsilon)
.
Obviously,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}

under the FDCM and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}
under the FICM.
Under FDCM we have that
\Pr(A_{1}=\cdots=A_{d})=1
, and then

\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)=(1-\varepsilon),\;\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)=\cdots=\delta_{d-1}(\varepsilon)=0
, and

\delta_{d}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
.
In that situation, the
distribution of
Z_{\varepsilon}
simplifies to

(1-\varepsilon)H_{0}+\varepsilon\Delta_{\bm{c}}
where

\Delta_{\bm{c}}
is the distribution which puts
all of its mass in the point
\bm{c}
. The FICM instead
assumes that
A_{1},\dots,A_{d}
are independent,
hence

\delta_{\ell}(\varepsilon)=\binom{d}{\ell}(1-\varepsilon)^{d-\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell},\quad\ell=0,1,\ldots,d\;.

We first present the proposition deriving the IFs of
\bm{\mu}_{z}(H)
,
\bm{V}(H)
,
\bm{\mu}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(H)
,
\bm{\Sigma}^{\bm{u}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(H)
, and
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}(H)
, which are the functionals corresponding to
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{z}
and
\bm{\widehat{V}}
from (
5
),
\bm{\widehat{\mu}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})
and
\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{\text{\tiny MCD}}(\bm{\widehat{u}}_{i})
in (
7
), and
\bm{\widetilde{\Sigma}}_{\bm{z}^{\perp}}
in (
9
).
The proofs of the following propositions can be found in
Centofanti et al. 2025
.

When there are no missing values we can write
the functional version
(\bm{V}(H),\bm{\mu}_{z}(H))
of
the minimizer of (
5
) as

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt\left(\bm{V}(H),\bm{\mu}_{z}(H)\right)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{V},\bm{\mu}_{z}}\E_{H}\left[\rho_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}(H)}\sqrt{\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{1,j}^{2}(H)\rho_{1}\left(\frac{z_{j}-\mu_{z,j}-\bm{u}^{T}\bm{v}_{j}}{\sigma_{1,j}(H)}\right)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\text{such that}\quad\bm{u}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{u}}\rho_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}(H)}\sqrt{\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{1,j}^{2}(H)\rho_{1}\left(\frac{z_{j}-\mu_{z,j}-\bm{u}^{T}\bm{v}_{j}}{\sigma_{1,j}(H)}\right)}\right)

(A.5)

where
\bm{u}=\left(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\right)^{T}

and
\bm{z}=\left(z_{1},\dots,z_{d}\right)^{T}\sim H
.
Here
\sigma_{1,j}(H)
and
\sigma_{2}(H)
are
estimating functionals corresponding to
the initial scale estimates of

r_{j}:=z_{j}-\mu_{z,j}-\bm{u}^{T}\bm{v}_{j}
and

t:=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{1,j}^{2}(H)\rho_{1}(r_{j}/\sigma_{1,j}(H))/d}
.
This is subject to the first-order conditions
in
Centofanti et al. 2026
given by

\displaystyle\E_{H}\left[\bm{W}\bm{V}\bm{u}\bm{u}^{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{H}\left[\bm{W}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{z})\bm{u}^{T}\right],

\displaystyle\E_{H}\left[\bm{W}\bm{V}\bm{u}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{H}\left[\bm{W}\left(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{z}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\left(\bm{V}^{T}\bm{W}\bm{V}\right)\bm{u}

\displaystyle=\bm{V}^{T}\bm{W}(\bm{z}-\bm{\mu}_{z})\,.

Here
\bm{W}=\diag(\bm{w})
for

\bm{w}=\left(w_{1},\dots,w_{d}\right)^{T}
.
The components of
\bm{w}
are
w_{j}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{j}w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}

with cellwise weights

w_{j}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\frac{r_{j}}{\sigma_{1,j}}\right)

and casewise weights

w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}=w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\frac{t}{\sigma_{2}}\right)
.
We also denote

\bm{\widetilde{W}}=\diag(w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{1},\dots,w^{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}_{d})
.

Proposition 6
.

The casewise and cellwise influence functions
of
\vect(\bm{V})
and
\bm{\mu}_{z}
are

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)=-\bm{D}_{1}\Big[\bm{S}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0}\right)+\bm{g}\left(\Delta_{\bm{c}},\vect\left(\bm{V}_{0}\right),\bm{\mu}_{z,0},\bm{\sigma}_{0}\right)\Big],

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}_{z},H_{0}\right)=-\bm{D}_{2}\Big[\bm{S}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0}\right)+\bm{g}\left(\Delta_{\bm{c}},\vect\left(\bm{V}_{0}\right),\bm{\mu}_{z,0},\bm{\sigma}_{0}\right)\Big],

and

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\bm{c},\vect(\bm{V}),H_{0}\right)=-\bm{D}_{1}\Big[\bm{S}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0}\right)+d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\bm{g}\left(H\left(j,\bm{c}\right),\vect\left(\bm{V}_{0}\right),\bm{\mu}_{z,0},\bm{\sigma}_{0}\right)\Big],

(A.6)

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\mu}_{z},H_{0}\right)=-\bm{D}_{2}\Big[\bm{S}\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}\left(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0}\right)+d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\bm{g}\left(H\left(j,\bm{c}\right),\vect\left(\bm{V}_{0}\right),\bm{\mu}_{z,0},\bm{\sigma}_{0}\right)\Big],

(A.7)

with
\bm{\sigma}(H)=\left(\sigma_{1,1}(H),\dots,\sigma_{1,d}(H),\sigma_{2}(H)\right)^{T}
,
\bm{\mu}_{z,0}:=\bm{\mu}_{z}(H_{0})
,

\bm{V}_{0}:=\bm{V}(H_{0})
,
\bm{\sigma}_{0}:=\bm{\sigma}(H_{0})
,

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{1}(H,\bm{\mu}_{z},\bm{V},\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\vect\left(\E_{H}\big[\bm{W}\right(\bm{V}\bm{u}-\bm{z}+\bm{\mu}_{z}\left)\bm{u}^{T}\big]\right),

(A.8)

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{2}(H,\bm{\mu}_{z},\bm{V},\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\E_{H}\big[\bm{W}\left(\bm{V}\bm{u}-\bm{z}+\bm{\mu}_{z}\right)\big],

(A.9)

and
\bm{g}(H,\bm{\mu}_{z},\bm{V},\bm{\sigma})=\left(\bm{g}_{1}(H,\bm{\mu}_{z},\bm{V},\bm{\sigma})^{T},\bm{g}_{2}(H,\bm{\mu}_{z},\bm{V},\bm{\sigma})^{T}\right)^{T}
.
The matrices
\bm{D}_{1}
,
\bm{D}_{2}
, and

\bm{S}
are described in
Centofanti et al. 2025
, and

\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{case}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0})
and
\IFu_{\mbox{\scriptsize{cell}}}(\bm{c},\bm{\sigma},H_{0})

are the casewise and cellwise influence functions of

\bm{\sigma}
.

Note that the solutions
\bm{V}(H)
and
\bm{\mu}_{z}(H)
of (
B
) are not unique. Therefore, the influence functions of
\vect(\bm{V})
and
\bm{\mu}_{z}
should be interpreted with respect to the functionals
\bm{V}(H)
and
\bm{\mu}_{z}(H)
defined as the output of the algorithm used to minimize (
5
), translated from the finite-sample setting to the population distribution setting and initialized at
\bm{V}_{0}
and
\bm{\mu}_{z,0}
.
Moreover, note that (
A.8
) and (
A.9
)
express two of the first order conditions, but
the other first-order condition
